Lemma 5.2 (Preparation and local evolution). There is an integer Jmax ≥ 1 and a canonical
preparation indexed by j = 0, . . . , Jmax with the following properties.
  (i) At j = 0 all frame-dependent full and A-only transient slots are zero. At j = Jmax all full
      increment programs and critical focuses are complete, and every full-target fill buffer and
      orbit-focus buffer is zero.
 (ii) For every field array b and frame c, every snapshot is uniquely specified. Its transition to the
      next or previous snapshot uses a fixed finite list of permutation-neighbors of each requested
      center.
 (iii) The number of steps is Jmax = O(Mo poly(r)). The number of stored slots, the schedule
       description, and the work to interpret one microclock or perform one local transition are
       poly(r), including entry dimensions and bit lengths.
 (iv) At full preparation, applying an edit hσ (c) changes the entire retained tuple to its canonical
      value on b + hσ (c) by local addition of the stored increment outputs. The public focus is
      unchanged.

Proof. Concatenate the public focus, all full-target filling schedules, and the critical-read focuses.
The stage lists for selectors and cutoffs have fixed padded lengths independent of their address values.
The per-target mode schedule consists of Mo iterations of fixed polylogarithmic sublength, together
with its initial and final buffer steps. The exceptional first iteration and final erasure are specified
separately. Thus a binary microclock can be interpreted by scanning the polynomial-in-r list of
program boundaries and performing arithmetic on O(log Mo + log poly(r)) bits. The interpreter
selects the current iteration, buffer, initialized prefix, and transition; it does not execute all preceding
iterations. Distinct programs may use distinct scratch indices to keep their shapes and twists fixed.
An idle preparation step supplies Jmax ≥ 1 if needed.
    For the last assertion, write Dσ (b) for the stored full increment of a positive-degree full slot D.
At the top perform, simultaneously,

                                D(b) 7−→ D(b) + Dσ (b) = D(b + hσ (c))                                (5.3)

for every persistent and transient full computation slot. Equation (5.3) is valid in its own encoder
because that encoder is linear. The increment slots themselves are included in this update whenever
their assigned degree is positive; their required increments exist by Lemma 5.1. Degree-zero slots do
not change. All additions read the old tuple before writing the new one.
    The full-target temporary buffers are already empty. Public focus depends only on c and remains
fixed. Each main critical-read stack is a linear selector-and-pull program at fixed public addresses.
Adding the matching focused increment stack therefore updates all its initialized stages to their new
canonical values. Those increment stacks have degree zero and stay fixed. This verifies the assertion
also for the retained A-only data. The preparation can now be erased in the reverse order, using
exactly the same recipes on the new b and unchanged frame.




                                                    42
5.3   The extended graph and its endpoint payload
Define an extended valid label to be

                    v = (c, b, η, j),     0 ≤ c < 4h,     η ∈ {0, 1},    0 ≤ j ≤ Jmax ,          (5.4)

where b is a valid macrograph state at stage c if η = 0, and at stage c + 1 (mod 4h) if η = 1. We
call these modes pre and post, respectively. Its raw word Rv contains, at every k ∈ Γ, all four parts
of the persistent tuple P (b), all full and A-only transient data in the canonical snapshot (c, b, j),
and a canonically encoded repeated copy of (c, η, j) and its range information. Padding conventions
will be fixed below.
    In pre mode increase j until the top. At the top use the prepared critical reads to compute the
macrograph’s forward choice and existence flag. If the edge exists, perform (5.3), leave c, j fixed,
and change the mode to post. In post mode decrease j until zero, then perform

                                  (c, b, 1, 0) 7−→ (c + 1 mod 4h, b, 0, 0).                      (5.5)

The reverse rules use the reverse preparation transition, the reverse macro choice at the same start
frame, and the inverse of (5.5). Both directions read their choice and flag before the top update. At
a missing edge the corresponding proposed word is defined to be the current raw word and its flag
is zero. This is a total proposal convention, not a graph self-edge. At a malformed input the local
proposal and flag likewise have a fixed total default.
    For each valid macro vertex at stage s, the extended graph contains the directed path

                     (s − 1, b, 1, Jmax ) −→ · · · −→ (s − 1, b, 1, 0)
                                                                                                 (5.6)
                                         −→ (s, b, 0, 0) −→ · · · −→ (s, b, 0, Jmax ),

with frames taken modulo 4h. Macro edges join the corresponding ends of these paths. Every
internal edge changes the clock or mode, and every top edge changes mode, so none is a self-edge.
The graph has indegree and outdegree at most one. Every label belongs to a path of positive length;
there are no isolated vertices.
    Let (s∗ , b∗ ) be the public exceptional macro source of Lemma 2.5. The unique exceptional
extended source and its actual successor are

                    v∗ = (s∗ − 1, b∗ , 1, Jmax ),     v∗+ = (s∗ − 1, b∗ , 1, Jmax − 1).          (5.7)

Their entire raw words are deterministically computable in poly(N ) time. Indeed the macro source
is public, all full encoders and program formulas are computable by global array algorithms, and
even explicitly following this one preparation takes only polynomially many steps.
    An extended endpoint can occur only at a top label of (5.6). Map it to its macro vertex by
retaining b and taking stage c + η (mod 4h). If it is not v∗ , the endpoint decoder of Lemma 2.5
returns a nonzero string y with P (S(y)) ̸= y or S(P (y)) ̸= y. Thus the endpoint payload consists
of the logical tapes and the repeated label; it does not require finding a history of the transient
computation.

5.4   A packed tester for valid represented vertices
Let Π contain identity, all forward and inverse A- and B-neighbor maps needed by the codes, the left
H generators used for payload independence, the right first-bit shifts and single-cycle rotations and
their inverses, and the right H generators used by scratch programs. Add the finitely many bounded


                                                     43
compositions required by a primitive local expression. Its cardinality q = |Π| is fixed independently
of N . Arbitrary powers of a rotation or paths through H are represented by successive stages; they
do not add new query maps to this list. At every center the tester reads W |Π(k) and rejects if any
applicable condition below fails:
  (i) canonical parsing, legal repeated clock, range checks, and zero padding;
 (ii) all full-slot local codes, A/B agreements, and systematic-payload duplication, including targets
      that are partial or inactive;
 (iii) all A-only local codes and A agreements, including inactive scratch;
 (iv) all structural persistent relations and the macro-validity conditions selected by (c, η);
 (v) every required current-snapshot relation: completed full-operation checks, the current partial-
     target comparison, initialized scratch-prefix recurrences in both buffers and all focuses, and
     inactive-zero checks.
In particular, the tester does not choose one relation uniformly from a growing list. All relations
share this fixed set of center permutations. Write rej(W ) for its rejection probability at uniform
k ∈ Γ, and use normalized whole-symbol distance

                                 dist(W, W ′ ) = Pr [W (k) ̸= W ′ (k)].
                                                 k∈Γ

Lemma 5.3 (Soundness of the valid-word tester). There are fixed constants Crep < ∞ and grep > 0
such that, if rej(W ) < grep , some valid extended label v satisfies

                                     dist(W, Rv ) ≤ Crep rej(W ).

The tester has perfect completeness. Conversely, rej(W ) ≤ q dist(W, Rv ) for every valid label v.
Distinct valid labels have raw distance at least a fixed δraw > 0.

Proof. Put θ = rej(W ). The parsing, repetition, and full-code proximity conclusions of Theorem 3.8
first change O(θ) centers to give legal constant clock data and exact full codewords. A corrected
repeated clock of illegal range would cause rejection everywhere, so small enough θ rules it out.
Exact one-axis distance then forces systematic-payload duplication in every full slot. Otherwise
that one failed duplication relation already has a fixed positive rejection probability. Zeroing locally
A-invalid scratch parts costs another O(θ) centers. A change on z centers changes any simultaneous
test on at most qz centers; this estimate uses the common permutation list and does not depend on
the number of slots.
    We next restore all scratch relative to these corrected full codewords and the corrected clock,
before using any advertised full-operation identity. The reference for the public focus is the actual
hardwired public word at the corrected frame. Treat its recurrences first. A later table-dependent
recurrence is active only when this reference focus is complete and hence gives identical correct
coefficients at every center. Each initial expression X in a mode-fill program is A-coded from
its exact full parents, even if those parents do not yet satisfy their advertised payload recipes.
The critical and increment focuses also start from exact A-coded full words. Thus every required
reference prefix is exactly A-coded.
    These are precisely the reference and ordering hypotheses of Lemma 4.3. The preprocessing
above changed only O(θ) sites, so the common query list bounds both the joint A-conflict fraction
and the simultaneous recurrence-failure fraction of this one preprocessed word by O(θ). Apply the
lemma to its entire scratch tuple, including inactive zeros. Its fixed-word induction keeps every

                                                  44
prefix estimate O(θ) and makes only one final replacement. All scratch is therefore restored at cost
O(θ), independently of the number of stages. No full-operation identity has been used to obtain
this conclusion.
    Call the resulting tuple W  f . Its remaining tests still fail with probability at most C ′ θ for a
fixed C ′ . By Lemma 4.5, either its persistent tuple is exactly P (b) for logical bits b valid at the
selected macro stage, or a persistent relation fails with fixed positive probability. Choose θ below
that gap divided by C ′ to obtain the former alternative. Now follow the initialization order of the
transient full targets. Completed targets with canonical parents are forced to their exact outputs
by the shared-axis gap following Definition 4.1. For the current partial target, its required cutoff
comparison has the same gap by Lemma 4.4. The cutoff scratch has already been restored: its
completed value is precisely Pt′ X. In the case that the target shares only the B axis with X, orbit
invariance makes this completed reference B-coded. Only at this point is B-axis distance applied.
No intermediate A-only scratch is assumed to be B-valid. Inactive full targets are forced to zero by
full-word distance.
    There are only finitely many kinds of these gaps, uniformly in their stage indices, program
lengths, and vector dimensions. Their minimum is positive. If any relation failed exactly on W     f , it
alone would give at least that gap in the packed test. Decrease θ once more to rule out every such
failure. All full slots and restored scratch then have the unique canonical values of (c, b, η, j). The
total restoration cost is Crep θ for a fixed Crep .
    Canonical snapshots satisfy every test by their construction, proving perfect completeness. If a
neighborhood avoids the disagreement set between W and Rv , its test accepts; a union bound over
Π proves the converse. Finally, two labels with different repeated data differ at every center. If
only their logical arrays differ, one logical singleton interpolant differs. Its full encoded difference
is nonzero and has the fixed distance of Theorem 3.8, including the constant conversion between
four-part and whole-symbol metrics. This gives δraw > 0.

5.5   Full representation cost and a fixed local Boolean exponent
We record the size argument before choosing any correction accuracy or any number of Boolean
ports. This order is needed later. All constants in the following count depend only on the fixed gate
basis, d2 , and the already chosen outer code parameters.
    One-bit alignment takes O(r) elementary rotations. A conjunction of bit conditions takes O(r2 )
primitive stages, and a carry or borrow shift takes O(r3 ). The Lagrange window product takes
O(r4 ) stages. The other transforms and masks in Section 4 fit within this last bound. There are
O(r) initial factor and row families: a fixed number of row lists, with O(r) routing layers for each
needed permutation and a bounded subset list for each monomial. Hence there are O(r5 ) persistent
primitive stages. Storing their ancestor graphs, parent indices, types, and coefficient instructions
is bounded, with ample slack, by Cr20 description bits. Public indexed field data are counted
separately as local constant words below; descriptors refer to their entries by indices, rather than
copying an entire length-h array into a descriptor.
    Here is an explicit finite-depth bound for derivative expansion. Let T bound the complete
current list of graph descriptions and subgraph descriptions, with each slot, parent occurrence, and
instruction counted at least once. A target graph has at most T nodes and at most T primary roots.
Substitution at one root uses a bounded number of the O(r4 ) Lagrange programs. There are at
most T target graphs. Recording an ancestor subgraph for every newly introduced stage, retaining
sharing within it and allowing fresh copies for separate targets, costs at most the square of the size
of its target computation. Public address instructions and index encodings contribute fixed powers



                                                   45
of r and logarithms of these list lengths. The following deliberately loose bound therefore covers all
these operations at one tier:

                               Ta+1 ≤ Cr20 (Ta + 1)6 ,      T0 ≤ Cr20 .                            (5.8)

For example, the six factors allow a separate factor for choosing a target, traversing its graph,
substituting its roots, and copying the ancestor descriptions, with two additional factors for indexing
and duplication; the actual substitution keeps more sharing. If

                            E0 = 20,      Ea+1 = 6Ea + 20,        E = Ed2 ,                        (5.9)

then all descriptions are O(rE ). This recurrence is composed only d2 times, by Lemma 5.1.
    For each full target, a cutoff program uses at most |H| group elements, r prefix rectangles, r
selectors per rectangle, and O(r) primitive alignment stages per selector. This is O(r12 ) stages. For
the orbit focus, there are at most |H| frequencies and |H| requested group indices. Even finding and
storing a separate generator path of length |H| for every such pull gives |H|3 stages, in addition to
the mode selectors. A bound O(r30 ) covers both focus and cutoff work and the two buffers. An
entry alphabet has dimension at most |H|2 ≤ r18 , and a field entry uses O(r) bits. Therefore

     raw data, local public constants, and schedule metadata at one center ≤ CrE+60 .             (5.10)

This includes public-address focus, critical focus, all slot types and all four parts. The long sequence
of Mo cutoffs is represented by a clock and reusable buffers, not by Mo copies of these data.
   Put ℓN = ⌈log2 (N + 2)⌉. Fix once an integer CL > E + 100 and pad each raw symbol to

                                         L = (ℓN + Cbase )CL .                                    (5.11)

A fixed lower bound on Cbase ≥ 1 makes (5.10) fit for every N . The unpadded construction is still
parameterized by r and the same slot schema. Increasing Cbase later adds zero padding only. The
raw word and all center-specific public descriptions have total length

                           |Γ| poly(ℓN ) = Mo |H| poly(ℓN ) = N poly(ℓN ).                        (5.12)

This is a bound on the listed bits, including index and field representations, rather than just the
number of slots.
    Every local test and proposal can now be implemented by a fixed polynomial-time bit algorithm
on a bounded number of L-bit symbols. Parsing, loops over slots and the explicit group H, arithmetic
in J, program interpretation, and multiplexing within a supplied polylogarithmic array use bounded
nests of polynomially bounded loops. The largest local computation is the orbit-restricted parity
solve of Lemma 4.4. Its dimension is at most

                                 C|O| |H| dimJ (U ) ≤ C|H|4 ≤ CL4 .

Its explicitly stored matrix has O(L9 ) bits if each field entry is bounded by L bits. Gaussian
elimination uses O(L12 ) field operations, and elementary arithmetic and inversion have a schoolbook
bound O(L4 ) in this generous bit-length allowance. Even accessing each entry by a linear scan
through the matrix, and scanning the full slot and metadata lists, adds only fixed polynomial factors.
All other displayed local operations have the same type of finite loop bound. No routine iterates
through the Mo cutoffs to answer one query. Choose an integer a exceeding the sum of these fixed
loop and arithmetic exponents. A standard direct time-unrolling of a deterministic bit computation
of length O(La ) gives an acyclic bounded-fanin Boolean circuit of size O(L2a+2 ): represent each

                                                  46
configuration by its used tape cells and head indicators, and implement each next configuration by
Boolean selection. Thus one fixed exponent craw bounds all raw local circuits. Its precise numerical
value is immaterial; the displayed construction fixes it before correction rounds or port arities are
selected.
    All lists and constants are uniformly constructible. Global algorithms enumerate the finite
fields, groups, public routing tables, and full constant encodings, using the deterministic searches
and linear algebra of Sections 3 and 4. Their time is poly(N ), though it need not be quasilinear.
Input-dependent data without succinct evaluation at c are accessed through (5.2); other public
constants are hardwired only at the bounded, publicly specified neighbors of the requested center.
There is no hidden access to an unrepresented length-N array.

5.6     Correction and the complete outer interface
All full and scratch slots of Rv satisfy their A constraints. Apply the joint A-axis corrector of
Lemma 3.9 to all of them, together with majority correction of the repeated clock and canonical
padding. The local decoder enumerates at most 2d label subsets and solves the common scalar con-
straints componentwise. Since d is fixed, this again has a fixed polynomial bound in L, independent
of the number or dimensions of the packed slots. Enlarge craw once to cover this one-round routine.
    Let Q ≥ 2 bound its neighborhood size and the sizes of the raw neighborhoods. For any fixed
nonnegative integer t, unrolling t correction rounds followed by a raw local routine uses at most
                                     C(1 + Q + · · · + Qt ) = O(Qt )                              (5.13)
copies of these same L-bit circuits. Their intermediate outputs still have length L. It follows that,
for a constant Ct depending on t, the composite circuit has size at most Ct Lcraw . Its ports are
the finitely many compositions of the original permutations to depth at most t, followed by a raw
neighborhood. The exponent is independent of t. A later increase of Cbase changes only the linear
work of checking and writing padding; it changes neither this exponent nor the number of ports. In
particular, fixed multiplicative constants may be absorbed into a subsequent exponent allowance
with slack by increasing Cbase after all those constants have been fixed.
Theorem 5.4 (Outer representation interface). There are fixed positive constants δraw , ρraw , fixed
integers CL , craw , and uniform deterministic algorithms with the following properties for every
End-of-Line input of length N . They construct a directed graph of valid extended labels, represented
by canonical words
      Rv ∈ ({0, 1}L )Γ ,     |Γ| = N poly(ℓN ),      L = (ℓN + Cbase )CL ,     ℓN = ⌈log2 (N + 2)⌉,
where Cbase may be any sufficiently large fixed integer.
  (i) Graph and endpoint data. The graph has indegree and outdegree at most one, no self-edges,
      and no isolated vertices. It has the public distinguished source v∗ and actual successor v∗+ in
      (5.7); their entire words are computable in poly(N ) time. Every other endpoint is mapped in
      poly(N ) time to a nonzero solution of the input End-of-Line instance. Every canonical word
      is computable from its valid label in polynomial time.
 (ii) Distance and valid-word testing. Distinct canonical words have normalized symbol distance
      at least δraw . A uniform center test has perfect completeness and uses only a fixed finite list Π
      of permutation-neighbors, of cardinality q independent of N . For every fixed α > 0 there is a
      fixed θα > 0 such that
                           rej(W ) < θα   =⇒      dist(W, Rv ) < α   for some valid v.

                                                    47
     Also rej(W ) ≤ q dist(W, Rv ) for every valid v.
 (iii) Local proposals and flags. At each requested center, total deterministic routines on Π(k)
       compute a next or previous proposed symbol and an existence bit. On Rv the bit is independent
       of the center and equals the actual edge-existence flag. If it is one, the proposed symbols form
       exactly the canonical neighboring word; if it is zero, the default proposal is Rv itself.
 (iv) Value-independent uncertainty correction. There is a total local one-round corrector
      with a bounded permutation neighborhood. If |U |/|Γ| ≤ ρraw , it maps every word agreeing
      with Rv outside U to a word agreeing with Rv outside a set U + determined only by U , with
      |U + | ≤ |U |/2. Consequently t rounds leave a prescribed uncertainty support of density at
      most 2−t |U |/|Γ|. The guarantee holds for arbitrary values on U . Different requested-center
      simulations may use different values there, provided repeated occurrences of a position are
      consistent within each individual cone.
 (v) Global recovery. From any word within ρraw symbol distance of a valid word, a deterministic
     polynomial-time algorithm recovers its exact extended label and raw word. It then recovers the
     logical bits by systematic coefficient extraction and evaluation on the subgroup. In particular it
     extracts an End-of-Line answer whenever the recovered label is a nonexceptional endpoint.
 (vi) Uniform cost and order of choices. The total raw data and listed public descriptions have
      N poly(ℓN ) bits and are constructible in poly(N ) time. Each raw local test, proposed symbol,
      flag, and one-round corrector has a Boolean circuit of size at most CLcraw on a fixed number
      of symbols. For each fixed t, correction for t rounds followed by any such raw routine has size
      at most Ct Lcraw and at most O(Qt ) symbol ports, with uniform construction for that t. The
      exponents CL , craw are fixed before t and any later port arity. Further fixed additive padding in
      Cbase has only linear processing cost and does not change these exponents or arities.

Proof. The graph and local evolution were established in Lemma 5.2 and (5.6). The tester and
distance are Lemma 5.3. For example, its constants allow

                                    θα = min{grep /2, α/(2Crep )}.

All local proposals use the same primitive permutation list as the tester, enlarging it by a fixed
number of maps if necessary.
    For the correction claim, let d and δ1 be the outer A-code degree and distance. Choose ρraw
smaller than both δraw /3 and δ1 /4, and smaller than the fixed uncertainty radius in Lemma 3.9. An
admissible choice of U + is the set of centers having at least (δ1 /2)d neighbors in U in a forward or
inverse A list. Off this set all row suggestions have fewer than half the local minimum distance in
errors, so the joint decoder recovers the reference exactly; the clock majority and padding agree
there too. If u = |U |/|Γ|, the spectral estimate from that lemma gives

                                      |U + |       2λ2 u      u
                                             ≤             2
                                                             ≤ .
                                       |Γ|     (δ1 /2 − u)    2

Neither this support nor the proof refers to the values inside U . For a requested-center cone with
consistent repeated positions, extend its initially supplied values arbitrarily to the whole word,
setting the reference outside U . The same support argument applies. This proves the assertion even
when separate cones choose different extensions.
    After O(log |Γ|) synchronous global rounds the uncertainty set has cardinality less than one,
giving exact recovery in polynomial time. From a recovered logical singleton slot, read its coefficient

                                                  48
payload and evaluate the degree-< h interpolant at ω j , 0 ≤ j < h, to obtain the corresponding
logical bits. The repeated clock gives (c, η, j). The distance choice makes the promised reference
unique. On inputs outside the recovery promise the algorithm has a total default; it may check the
decoded label and its canonical word by the global polynomial-time algorithms. Finally, (5.8)–(5.12)
and (5.13) give all asserted cost and parameter-order statements.


6      Binary composition and the robust outer circuit
We now convert the symbol representation into a generalized circuit. Two features of the conversion
require separate arguments. A local Boolean function may inspect polynomially many bits of
a bounded number of symbols; its implementation must tolerate failures among its own gates.
Moreover, sharing such implementations must not allow one corrupted physical value to spoil an
unbounded number of decisions. We state the first interface, proved in Section 9, and then give
the construction and all the counting and synchronization arguments for the second. The same
sequence is used inside a decision gadget in Section 9: first obtain replica synchronization without
trusting decisions, then bound unstable comparisons and failed computations, and finally use the
clipped mean displacement to locate an endpoint. At the outer scale, a completed decision is shared
by a group of output replicas, called a cohort; its internal gates and external read occurrences are
charged separately from each output’s own remaining gates.
    Throughout this section, a distance between words is relative Hamming distance, unless a norm
is displayed. A constant may depend on previously fixed accuracies, but never on the End-of-Line
input or its length. A “fixed branch” of a randomized answer tree means that the earlier hypothetical
answers are fixed when the randomized algorithm is described. It does not mean conditioning
the probability space on receiving those answers. This distinction applies also to branches never
executed.

6.1     A fixed port and a robust Boolean decision
The port family will encode every raw symbol in the same binary format. Its tester lets us recognize
symbols close enough to use, and its recovery radius is fixed before we know how many symbols a
corrected local computation will inspect. Once that arity is fixed, the robust-decision part of the
interface realizes one Boolean output of such a computation. It provides many designated copies
of the answer, so the guarantee remains useful even when failed gates concentrate on designated
outputs.

Theorem 6.1 (Standardized ports and robust decisions). For every fixed positive integer exponent
allowance callow , there are a cutoff Lport and a uniform family of injective binary encodings, for
L ≥ Lport ,
                             D : {0, 1}L −→ {0, 1}S0 ,   S0 = poly(L),
with fixed positive relative distance dB , satisfying (i)– (ii) below. The radius νB in (iii) is fixed with
this port family; the actual-gadget assertions in that part have the separate later cutoff specified
there.
    (i) Every coordinate of D(s) is a multilinear polynomial in the bits of s over F2 , of degree at
        most 10. Its coefficient list is computable in time polynomial in L. The degree bound is
        independent of callow . Encoding and exact recovery from a fixed positive relative-error radius
        are deterministic polynomial-time algorithms.



                                                    49
 (ii) A perfect-completeness tester makes a bounded number of bit queries and performs bounded
      Boolean answer processing. Its public randomness has an enumerable polynomial-size rational
      support and polynomial bit complexity. For every fixed a > 0, there are a fixed θB (a) > 0 and
      a cutoff LB (a) ≥ Lport , allowed to depend on a, such that for every L ≥ LB (a) rejection below
      θB (a) implies distance less than a from D({0, 1}L ). A word at distance a from an encoding
      has rejection at most CB a, for a fixed CB . Each fixed-branch query has a bounded-density
      marginal relative to the uniform distribution on the block. These assertions include arbitrary
      words and all hypothetical branches.
 (iii) There is a constant 0 < νB < dB /4 determined with this encoding, before any arity is specified.
       Subsequently fix an arity bound k, a size factor Cf , and a desired output loss υ > 0. There
       is a further cutoff Lg , allowed to depend on these fixed choices, such that all actual-gadget
       assertions below hold for every L ≥ max{Lport , Lg }. Consider any single-bit Boolean function

       public 0L .
     There is a uniform generalized subcircuit for this function, with a common count scale G0 =
     poly(L) for all padded functions under these bounds, at most Cg G0 gates, and a set of W
     designated output wires produced by its gates, where

                                               cg G0 ≤ W ≤ Cg G0 .                                      (6.1)

      Here cg , Cg > 0 are fixed. Each nonpublic external input is a block of physical reads X = 3x − 1,
      with x ∈ [0, 1], compared to a public common rational threshold ξ ∈ [2/5, 3/5]. Every external
      comparison occurrence in the complete unfolded subcircuit can be routed separately to an
      external replica. For each external port position, the total number of these occurrences is at
      most
                                                Cocc G0 /S0 ,                                     (6.2)
      uniformly over ξ, the function, and its public parameters. This counts all hypothetical branches,
      including unsuccessful and default branches.
     There are fixed δg , γg , εg > 0 with the following universal soundness property. Fix logical strings
     s1 , . . . , sk and ideal binary port words yj satisfying dist(yj , D(sj )) ≤ νB for each nonpublic
     port. For any subsequently chosen fixed physical margin ω > 0, use

                                          0 < ε ≤ εg ,      ε < ω/1000.

      Suppose at most γg G0 external comparison occurrences fail to have a physical value on the side
      of ξ specified by the corresponding bit of yj , at gap greater than ω. Repeated occurrences need
      not have consistent physical values. Then every assignment of the internal nodes in [0, 1] with
      at most δg G0 failed gates makes all but υW designated wires correct near-bits for f (s1 , . . . , sk ):
      at most ε for zero and at least 1 − ε for one.
The port construction and νB are independent of k, Cf , υ. The later constants, the gate-count bounds,
and the topology bounds may depend on these fixed choices, but not on L, ξ, or the later margin
ω. The threshold may appear as a rational gate parameter. Construction and other parameter
computations take polynomial time in L and the supplied description lengths, including the bit
length of ξ; all parameter lengths are bounded by a polynomial in these quantities. In particular an
arbitrarily long externally supplied threshold is charged for its bits.

    Sections 7 and 8 construct the algebraic and binary ingredients. Proposition 9.1 proves the
fixed encoding, tester, and recovery assertions. The remaining construction in Section 9 proves the

6.2   Port substitution, validation, and average correction
Apply Theorem 5.4, writing its raw encoding as Rw = (sk )k∈Γ , with
                                                                                   O(1)
                ℓN = ⌈log2 (N + 2)⌉,       L = (ℓN + Cbase )CL ,       |Γ| = O(N ℓN       ).

Choose callow > craw + 12, with any additional fixed local polynomial overhead absorbed in this
inequality, and construct D before choosing correction rounds. Put

                                 Ew = (D(sk ))k∈Γ ,        m = |Γ|S0 .                            (6.3)

The product of the two relative distances is a positive Hamming-distance bound; write d∗ > 0 for
its square root, a lower bound on normalized Euclidean separation. The distinguished source and
its actual successor are publicly computable in polynomial time.
    There is a fixed radius ρdec > 0 from which these words can be decoded globally. Indeed, let
rB > 0 be a port recovery radius and rraw > 0 a raw recovery radius. If the whole binary distance is
less than rB rraw /2, at most an rraw /2 fraction of port blocks can be at distance at least rB from
their true block. Decode all ports, using a fixed default on an invalid or unsuccessful decoder output,
and then use the raw decoder. An incorrect decoding at a bad site need not be detectable; the raw
error bound already charges all such sites. Fix a smaller radius ρdec if necessary.
    The graph in Theorem 5.4 has indegree and outdegree at most one, no self-edges, and no isolated
vertices. Write w∗ for its distinguished source, which has an actual successor, and call every other
missing-neighbor vertex a desired endpoint. The geometric construction applies to this encoded
graph. For an edge u → v, its four-block polygon successively changes the second, third, first, and
third blocks:
                          (Eu , Eu , 0, 0) −→ (Eu , Ev , 0, 0) −→ (Eu , Ev , 1, 0)
                                        −→ (Ev , Ev , 1, 0) −→ (Ev , Ev , 0, 0).
Here a scalar in a block denotes its constant repetition. The corners are rounded, and a segment in
the fourth block enters the distinguished source from (Ew∗ , Ew∗ , 0, 2). Theorem 10.1 constructs a
displacement field on
                                                            1 X 2
                               B = [−1, 2]4m ,     ∥U ∥2b =       Ui ,
                                                            m
                                                                   i

where all distances to subsets of B use this norm. Each point of its endpoint set E has first block
exactly Ew for a desired endpoint w; the top of the extra segment is excluded. For any sufficiently
small fixed rational τ > 0, chosen to meet the validation and global-recovery bounds in (6.6), there
are constants B1 , c∗ , β0 > 0 and a field v such that, after increasing B1 to an integer,

                      ∥v(U )∥∞ ≤ B1 − 1,       ∥clipB (U + βv(U )) − U ∥b ≥ c∗ β                  (6.4)

whenever dist(U, E) > 10τ and 0 < β ≤ β0 . These constants precede estimator accuracy and the
step β. The circuit will keep many replicas of each physical coordinate and force their mean vector
close to E; rounding the first block will then permit global recovery.


                                                   51
    To approximate the field locally, the numerical procedure of Lemma 10.8 rounds the first two
blocks at one common threshold ξ ∈ [2/5, 3/5]. Each rounded word is a seed for validation. On
good decisions an accepted seed must identify one fixed reference vertex, independent of the random
acceptance decision and of the requested confidence or numerical accuracy; a seed in the intermediate
region may be accepted or rejected. If one of the first two physical blocks is within 5τ of a valid
word, its seed must be accepted with that reference on good decisions for every allowed threshold.
The procedure then asks for incident-edge flags, bits of each reference and its actual neighbors, and
bits of the distinguished source and its successor. Each word-bit request uses either the coordinate
matching the field component being estimated or a preallocated uniform coordinate for a bounded
scalar statistic. Each reference and its neighbors determine a bounded list of incident path pieces; a
fixed mesh and estimates of their scalar data approximate the field near those pieces. Scalar reads
use fixed-width quantization with a public offset. We next supply these calls. Their number is fixed
once the geometric mesh, scalar sample counts and numerical tolerances have been chosen, before
the smaller bit-error budgets are assigned.

Lemma 6.2 (Protecting functional inputs). There are constants 0 < a1 < a2 < νB , fixed before the
arity of any functional invocation, with the following property. A bounded Boolean flag routine on
an arbitrary binary port block can have arbitrarily small fixed failure probability, unflagging every
block within a1 of an encoding and flagging every block at distance at least a2 from all encodings.
In a functional invocation replace flagged ports by public 0L . Except on the union of the completed
flag routines’ failure events, every nonpublic input of every selected invocation is within νB of its
intended encoding.

Proof. Choose a2 < νB and then a1 so small that CB a1 < θB (a2 )/4. Estimate port-tester rejection
by a fixed number of independent trials and compare to θB (a2 )/2. Bounded independent variables
give any fixed confidence by increasing the fixed sample count. In the interval between the two
promises either Boolean result is permitted. An unflagged block on the good event is within a2 of
an encoding, unique because a2 < dB /4; take its logical string as the intended input. A flagged
input is explicitly a public zero, so it requires no external near-port premise. A union bound is
applied to the finite list of completed flag promises, after that list has been fixed. Their internal
tester answer trees are not counted as new logical promises.

    Fix a physical vector U and initially fix its rounding threshold and scalar offset. A rounded seed
y = (yk )k∈Γ is now a fixed binary word; use bit one at equality with the threshold. For analysis,
define a global nearest-symbol convention s∗ (k) at every site within a2 of a port encoding, and
choose a fixed arbitrary string elsewhere. An ideal functional call computes its Boolean function
on these symbols at unflagged inputs and on 0L at flagged inputs. Within one correction cone,
deduplicate repeated raw sites and share their flag and initial logical value. Different cones may
make different choices at uncertain sites.

Lemma 6.3 (Binary validation). There are fixed 0 < ρcomp < ρacc , with ρacc smaller than the
uniqueness radius of (6.3), and a finite ideal Boolean validation procedure such that, with arbitrarily
high fixed probability, acceptance implies distance less than ρacc from a unique valid Ew , and distance
less than ρcomp implies acceptance with the true reference. The procedure uses port flags, port tests,
and bounded raw-checker functional calls. It remains valid with averaged stationary expander-walk
sampling as described below.




                                                  52
Proof. Choose ρacc small enough that ρacc /a1 is within the raw uncertainty-correction radius. Choose
α < ρacc /4 and take the raw gap 0 < θα ≤ 1 from Theorem 5.4. Writing q = |Π| for its bounded
checker-neighborhood size, choose 0 < a < a1 and b0 > 0 satisfying

                    α + a + b0 < ρacc ,     qb0 < θα /16,         pmax = b0 θB (a) > 0.           (6.5)

Decrease pmax if necessary. Let p be the average port-tester rejection over all sites. Estimate p, and
independently simulate the raw checker at sampled centers using the flagged/substituted inputs.
Accept when the first estimate is at most pmax /2 and the simulated raw rejection estimate is at
most θα /2.
    On statistics of error less than pmax /4, acceptance gives p < pmax . Consequently the fraction of
sites not within a of any port encoding is less than b0 . At a center whose entire neighborhood avoids
these sites, good flag promises leave all inputs unflagged and make the simulated checker exactly the
checker on the global word s∗ . Exceptional centers occupy at most qb0 . In the analysis, also estimate
the true checker result on s∗ to accuracy θα /8 and the exceptional-center fraction to accuracy θα /16.
These are fixed bounded statistics; the algorithm need not compute the hypothetical true statistic.
On their good events and the completed flags’ good events,

                   Pr[raw checker rejects s∗ ] ≤ θα /2 + θα /8 + qb0 + θα /16 < θα .

Raw soundness makes s∗ α-close to a valid Rw . Charging all raw-disagreement sites and all
non-a-close sites at cost one yields

                                   dist(y, Ew ) < α + a + b0 < ρacc .

The uniqueness radius makes this reference independent of subsequent random acceptance decisions
for the fixed y.
    Conversely, if dist(y, Ew ) < ρcomp , then p ≤ CB ρcomp . By Markov’s inequality at most ρcomp /a
of the ports are at distance at least a from their true block. Outside their Π-neighborhoods the
simulated checker is the perfectly accepting raw checker. Choose ρcomp so small that both means
are strictly below one quarter of their acceptance thresholds; choose the sampling errors with the
remaining slack. Flag error probabilities are set after the sample counts. This proves both assertions.
The proof requires concentration of the sampled exceptional fraction when p is small. It never
requires an arbitrarily long batch to avoid every exceptional site.

Lemma 6.4 (Average correction of every relevant word role). After fixing the high-level mesh
and statistical call counts, boundedly many raw correction rounds suffice to supply every required
incident flag and every requested encoding bit, with any prescribed fixed average error. The average
is over a uniform output component or its uniform-marginal statistical requests. The conclusion
holds unconditionally for every true reference that can be accepted on good validation decisions,
before selecting pieces or numerical winners. All resulting single-bit functions fit the fixed exponent
allowance of Theorem 6.1.

                                                     53
Proof. Fix a rounded seed that can be accepted on good validation, and its unique reference
Rw = (sk ). Put
                    U0 = {k : dist(yk , D(sk )) > a1 }, |U0 |/|Γ| ≤ ρacc /a1 .
Outside U0 , good flags unflag the port and its unique nearest logical string is sk . Within U0 ,
substitution and intermediate flag choices may supply arbitrary values. In each deduplicated cone
extend those initial values to a whole raw word agreeing with Rw outside U0 . The raw uncertainty
theorem gives a residual set Ut , depending only on U0 , with arbitrarily small fixed density after
sufficiently many fixed rounds t. Its guarantee holds simultaneously for every such extension. Thus
different cones and correlated intermediate flags cause no additional exceptional support. A corrected
symbol is right off Ut ; a local proposal and edge flag are right off a fixed permutation expansion of
Ut .
     Compute an edge flag at a uniform-marginal center. For a requested own or neighboring encoding
bit, compute the appropriate raw output symbol and then the requested coordinate polynomial
of D. Matching coordinates are uniform in the output average; the statistical requests have the
same center marginal. Each fixed permutation expansion therefore costs at most its size times

and padding work has a fixed polynomial exponent. Our initial choice of callow leaves slack for this
work. The number of raw ports and the multiplicative factor may increase with t, but are now fixed.
The port encoding and its radius do not change.

    The completed port flags are now amplified for the finite list of logical promises in validation
and these correction cones. Conditional on a sampled site, they use fresh tester randomness. Make
the probability that any selected invocation has a nonpublic input outside νB arbitrarily small. This
protection concerns arbitrary rounded seeds, including seeds undergoing validation, and does not
require exclusion of the geometric endpoint neighborhood. Uncertain flag results only choose which
already uncertain inputs are substituted.

6.3   Small-support ideal tapes and complete branch counts
Lemma 6.5 (Averaged expander sampling). The statistics needed above can be sampled with
polynomial-in-L random support per output coordinate, with any desired fixed confidence in the
average over output coordinates.

Proof. Write an output coordinate as (b, k0 , z0 ) with b ∈ [4], k0 ∈ Γ, and z0 ∈ [S0 ]. Use fixed-length
labeled walks in the outer Cayley expander starting at k0 . Fresh bounded segments and auxiliary
uniform positions in [S0 ] supply all center and word statistics. Averaging over uniform k0 makes
every walk position stationary. If the mean-zero operator norm is at most λ < 1, bounded centered
functions at times s, t have covariance at most Cλ|s−t| . Independent auxiliary randomness only
changes the bounded diagonal terms and replaces off-diagonal functions by their conditional means.
Therefore a batch of qs samples satisfies
                                         

                           Var         Zj ≤ 2

Chebyshev’s inequality and a finite union give any fixed confidence by choosing a sufficiently large
fixed qs . The argument permits distinct bounded functions at the different slots and separate
batches for physical blocks.
    Apply it to port rejection, the fixed global raw-checker and exceptional indicators in validation,
and each actual reference candidate’s statistics in the geometric procedure. At fixed rounded seeds
those reference vertices are fixed whenever good acceptance is possible. Analyze all their roles before
a numerical minimum or uncertain acceptance chooses which ones to use. The concentration is not
conditioned on the realized answer path. Each walk has a fixed number of fixed-degree label choices.
Each remaining choice has polynomial-in- L support, and only a fixed number of choices is used.
Their joint support per output coordinate is consequently polynomial in L.

    The preceding routines now meet the local-access contract of Definition 10.6. Validation supplies
fixed unique references, average correction supplies the matching and statistical bit requests, and
the walk lemma supplies concentration for each underlying true candidate. The geometric procedure
reserves at most eight true word roles and thirty-two piece roles, as enumerated in Section 10.5.
Missing roles are omitted; duplicate roles may be retained. For fixed U outside the endpoint
neighborhood and each fixed admissible binary and scalar-quantization offset, it gives arbitrarily
                            vi − vi (U )|2 , with i uniform among the 4m components. All bit and flag
small prescribed 4Ei,trial |b
errors and all candidate statistics are analyzed before a numerical winner is selected. On the
resulting good event, Lemma 10.10 controls every sufficiently near-minimizing candidate in each
component before selection, so a winner correlated with the requested component is harmless. The
needed bit-error bounds remain average bounds over the allocated requests.
    Apart from the supplied calls, the procedure uses bounded comparison and Boolean-answer
processing, public rational numerical tables, and a signed dyadic output in [−B1 , B1 ], including on
every bad branch. A fixed-width scalar-quantization grid with an independently randomized offset
suffices. Refining the finite grids of offsets changes comparison thresholds, not the number of bins,
answers, or slots. All these statements concern the unscaled field.
    This outer application uses the accuracy and finite-role clauses (i)–(iii) of Lemma 10.8 with
completed ideal Boolean functional calls. Its optional physical-read clause (iv) is not applied to the
full outer gadget expansion. The fixed count Hout below counts direct outer read slots, and Bout
counts ideal functional invocations. Theorem 6.1 and Lemma 6.7 separately bound every physical
read created by substituting those invocations.
    Applying parts (i)–(iii) of Lemma 10.8 at this ideal-oracle level, with the preceding accuracy
choices, we obtain ideal dyadic outputs satisfying, for every eligible fixed U and every fixed admissible
offset,
                                               vi − vi (U )|2 < (c∗ κ/100)2 .
                                     4Ei,tape |b                                                    (6.7)
All outputs remain in [−B1 , B1 ] on bad trials. The good-port failure probability can independently
be made arbitrarily small on this high-level call list. It is useful to record the order explicitly:
 (1) Fix geometric roles, mesh, scalar counts and numerical tolerances.
 (2) Choose validation confidence and raw correction rounds for this finite logical-call list; fix the
     number of ports in every cone.
 (3) Amplify each completed flag for its prescribed failure probability. Amplification adds tester
     reads, not new logical promises.
 (4) Only now unfold the complete outer answer tree, leaving functional calls unexpanded and
     obtaining a fixed number Hout of direct read slots and Bout of hypothetical functional invoca-
     tions.

                                                   55
  (5) Choose υ for these Bout invocations and apply Theorem 6.1 with the now fixed arity and size
      factor.
This order does not define a bit accuracy by a union bound over the very answer tree created by
amplifying that accuracy.
    At the ideal-oracle level, unfold every flag pattern, substitution pattern, quantized bin and
hypothetical answer. Functional-call nodes remain unexpanded: each contributes one completed
Boolean oracle answer, and its physical implementation is separately accounted for below. Thus Hout
counts only direct read slots and Bout counts functional invocations. Supply randomness to unused
nodes too. Earlier answers that determine an invocation’s function or addresses are fixed publicly
in that copy; they are not additional fragile input wires. Thus all port functions remain Boolean
functions on their declared raw ports. A wrong numerical answer selects another reserved role or a
finite table case. It cannot become an arbitrary hidden word address. Each hypothetical raw site is
π(k0 ) for π in a fixed finite set P: compose walk-label moves, raw-neighborhood permutations and
correction-cone moves. This set is independent of later offset-grid refinement. Direct scalar and bit
slots use matching z0 , independently sampled uniform within-block positions, or the port tester’s
smooth queries. For fixed k0 and fixed offsets, summing their unconditioned position densities over
the finite complete tree gives a constant.
    The tape law in the next lemma includes both late offset grids—the binary threshold and the
independent scalar-quantization offset—together with all internal choices at the hypothetical nodes.
The grids are fixed before this final law is rationalized. The preceding accuracy estimates hold for
each fixed offset, and hence for this joint law; the finite grids multiply its support bound only by a
fixed factor.
Lemma 6.6 (Rational tapes and their read loads). The true tape law can be replaced deterministically,
at each output coordinate, by a list of a common square length Ttap = poly(L), with total variation
at most η0 /S0 , for any prescribed fixed η0 > 0. The lists use only choices in the original support.
They preserve (6.7) and any bounded rare-event estimate with arbitrarily small fixed slack. Across
these lists, every base physical coordinate is read by at most CTtap direct comparison slots in the
complete unfolded outer trees.
Proof. Let Q(L) bound the enumerable rational support of every tape law. For a common square
T ≥ Q(L)S0 /η0 , round each mass T pj down to an integer and distribute the remaining copies
among nonzero support entries. Each mass error is at most 1/T , and total variation is at most
Q(L)/T ≤ η0 /S0 . Floors, remainders and the ordering of entries are computed with exact rational
arithmetic in polynomial time. The probability descriptions have polynomial bit complexity. A
function in [0, A] changes expectation by at most A times total variation, proving the bounded-
statistic assertions.
      For the load assertion, explicitly list all potential direct slots A, including their fixed earlier-
answer paths; set H = |A|. If a slot is omitted or supplied a public value, its read subdistribution
is dominated by that of its potential slot; it is never renormalized on being selected. Fix a target
(b′ , k ′ , z ′ ) and a possible π ∈ P. Its preimage k0 = π −1 (k ′ ) is fixed. For every fixed offset, the true
law gives
                                  XS0
                                      E[number of direct reads at (b′ , k ′ , z ′ )] ≤ C,                   (6.8)
                             z0 =1
where C is the sum of the unconditioned fixed-slot density constants. Matching coordinates
contribute one, uniform sampled coordinates contribute their bounded counts, and tester queries
contribute their smoothness constants. Branches and seed roles add only a fixed factor. A position-
count random variable lies in [0, H]. Changing the law for one output by total variation η0 /S0 adds

                                                       56
at most Hη0 /S0 to its expectation; summing over z0 adds only Hη0 . Sum the fixed preimages and
physical roles and multiply by Ttap . This is the asserted O(Ttap ) load. A constant total-variation
tolerance without the S0 denominator would not give this bound.

    The occurrence bound (6.2) for each substituted gadget uses the same full-tree principle at its
own scale. Its tape approximation must control loads both on its internal word, of length min , and
on an external port, of length S0 . Section 9 therefore uses total variation η/ max{4min , S0 }; the
calculations (9.14)–(9.15) give O(G0 /S0 ) external occurrences per port position and O(Rin ) internal
reads per base coordinate, where Rin is the gadget’s replica count and G0 = 4min Rin . This accounts
for unequal polynomial block lengths without conditioning on an executed branch.

6.4   Cohorts, individual read routing, and gate realization
At each base component i ∈ [4m], allocate one cohort for every tape in its list. A cohort has Kc
output replicas, where Kc is a square satisfying

                                    max{1, Cg G0 } ≤ Kc ≤ CK G0                                   (6.9)

for a fixed CK ; increasing Cg if necessary also covers all designated-wire counts. The total number of
replicas per component is R = Ttap Kc , again a square. Each cohort contains its complete bounded
list of functional invocations, sharing these invocations among its Kc outputs. For each invocation,
assign its W designated wires to these outputs by round robin. Since W ≤ Kc ,

                       #{uses of any designated wire} ≤ ⌈Kc /W ⌉ ≤ 2Kc /W.                      (6.10)

A fraction υ of wrong designated wires therefore affects at most 2υ of the cohort outputs. The
remaining gates, called a direct cone, are separately allocated for each output replica.

Lemma 6.7 (Bounded physical read multiplicity). Every physical replica can be made the input of at
most a fixed number of comparison occurrences, counting all outer and inner hypothetical branches.
The construction is deterministic and uses no shared rounded bit between different comparison
occurrences.

Proof. Fix a physical block and a raw site k ′ . For each of the fixed possible address permutations,
a functional port requesting k ′ has a fixed k0 preimage. There are S0 output bit positions and
Ttap tapes at that preimage, and only a bounded number of invocation/port roles. Thus at most
CS0 Ttap invocation ports request k ′ . By (6.2), any position in that site is used at most

                                            Cocc G0
                                 CS0 Ttap           ≤ C ′ Ttap Kc = C ′ R                       (6.11)
                                              S0
times. Lemma 6.6, followed by replication over the cohort, gives O(Ttap Kc ) = O(R) direct comparison
occurrences at each base coordinate. Enumerate all these occurrences and assign successive ones
round robin to its R physical replicas. Each actual replica has bounded resulting multiplicity. Each
comparison is performed in its own gate cone directly on that replica’s x value. In particular, an
error in one comparison wire is not broadcast to other inner cones.

    We specify the direct cones using exactly the gate types in Definition 1.2. This also specifies
how public constants and finite numerical processing are charged. Every needed constant node is
the fresh output of a Constant gate in the cone using it, unless its value is incorporated as a legal
Scale parameter or into a Boolean truth table. A physical comparison at threshold t ∈ [−1, 2] is a
Constant gate with parameter (t + 1)/3 and a Less gate on the physical replica’s x node and that
constant, with orientation chosen for the required bit. Outside the physical range its answer is a
public constant. On a satisfied comparison, an ideal mean gap greater than 4ω, an actual read
deviation at most ω, and ε < ω/1000 leave a strict Less antecedent even after the constant error:
the node-scale gap is at least ω − ε > ε.
    Boolean finite tables, flag processing, and quantization-bin processing use And, Or and Not with
fan-in at most two. If their inputs are correct near-bits, each gate produces the same correct near-bit
interval, without accumulating Boolean error. In particular the weak inequalities at ε and 1 − ε are
sufficient. A selection among hypothetical invocations is implemented as a Boolean multiplexer:
And each candidate answer with its selector and Or the resulting terms. An inactive selector is a
low near-bit and forces that term low even if the unused candidate answer is arbitrary in [0, 1]. Thus
unsuccessful unused branches are shielded, without assuming that their outputs are bits. All finite
answer tables have fixed size, although their public entries may depend on the tape and threshold.
    Represent the ideal dyadic field value by

                          vb = B1 (p∗ − n∗ ),    p∗ , n∗ ∈ [0, 1],   p∗ n∗ = 0,                 (6.13)

using a fixed number of binary digits; enlarge the dyadic precision within the earlier estimator slack.
Scale the near-bit digits by their dyadic weights and combine them by clipped Add gates to obtain
p, n. Compute the x-average by sixteen Scale gates of weight 1/16 and a bounded Add chain. All
exact partial sums are in [0, 1]. Choose a positive rational step with tβ = βB1 /3 ≤ 1. Scale p, n
by tβ , add the positive term to the computed average, then subtract the negative term. The last
output, or a final Copy output, is xir itself. When the ideal signs are exclusive this expression is
exactly
                                    1 + clip[−1,2] ((Arep X)ir + βb
